```sh
  login_pid=$!
  sleep 0.15
  s3out=$(python3 "$probe" s3 "$s3_port" REPROKEY reprosecret us-east-1) || exit 2
  conn=$(python3 "$probe" connect "$s3_port") || exit 2
  wait "$login_pid"
```

Output, exit status 1:

```
  -- s3 port 59318, management port 59319, loop pool = default (FLYOLOGY_LOOP_POOL_SIZE unset)
  -- baseline: signed S3 ListBuckets with no other traffic (3 runs)
     run 1: 0.007 200 184  (seconds status bytes)
     run 2: 0.002 200 184  (seconds status bytes)
     run 3: 0.002 200 184  (seconds status bytes)
  -- worst baseline S3 latency: 0.007s
  -- one management login on its own (3 runs)
     run 1: 1.124 200 {"authenticated":true}
     run 2: 1.014 200 {"authenticated":true}
     run 3: 1.018 200 {"authenticated":true}
  -- threshold for a concurrent S3 request: 0.07s (max of 10x baseline and 0.050 s)
  -- S3 request issued 0.15 s into a valid login (3 runs)
     run 1: s3=0.883 200 184  tcp-connect=0.001 connected  login=1.036 200 {"authenticated":true}
     run 2: s3=0.862 200 184  tcp-connect=0.001 connected  login=1.017 200 {"authenticated":true}
     run 3: s3=0.853 200 184  tcp-connect=0.001 connected  login=1.014 200 {"authenticated":true}
  -- worst S3 latency during a login: 0.883s
  FAILED: an S3 request issued during an administrator login stays within 0.07s
  -- control: S3 request after the logins: 0.002 200 184  (seconds status bytes)
  ok: control: with no login in flight the same signed S3 request is fast
BUG REPRODUCED: 1 failed expectation(s)
```

The control shows the request is fast again once no login is in flight, so the signature, the probe and the listener are all healthy. The TCP connect is unaffected in every run, because the kernel accept queue absorbs the connection while no handler can run.

A second measurement against the same binary shows what happens with more than one login outstanding, and what a larger loop pool does:

```
== default loop pool (FLYOLOGY_LOOP_POOL_SIZE unset)
  -- baseline s3: 0.007 200 184
  -- ten concurrent logins:
     s3 at t+0.3 s: 9.780 200 184
     flood wall: 10.16s, statuses:   10 200
== FLYOLOGY_LOOP_POOL_SIZE=8
  -- baseline s3: 0.002 200 184
  -- one concurrent login:
     s3 at t+0.3 s: 0.002 200 184
     flood wall: 1.07s, statuses:    1 200
  -- ten concurrent logins:
     s3 at t+0.3 s: 0.798 200 184
     flood wall: 2.23s, statuses:   10 200
```

Ten simultaneous logins hold one S3 request for 9.780 s of the flood's 10.16 s, and all ten are run to completion. Raising the pool to eight groups removes the single-login stall, since the S3 probe then measures 0.002 s, but ten logins still stall an S3 request for 0.798 s. A larger pool raises the number of simultaneous derivations needed to starve the loop from one to the pool size and does nothing else.

Host: macOS 26.6.2, arm64, GNAT 16.1.0 via Alire. The binary is the crate's default `alr -n build` development profile. The host was on AC power with `pmset -g` reporting `powermode 0` and `pmset -g therm` recording no thermal or performance warning; every baseline and the comparison beside it were taken back to back under that profile.

## What should happen

An administrator sign-in must not change S3 request latency. `server/README.md:8-12` presents the two listeners as bounded services coupled only through supervision order, and `flyology-io-structured_servers.ads:98-102` requires CPU-only handler code to poll `Cancellation.Requested` cooperatively, which a 600,000-iteration derivation with no poll does not.

## Suggested treatment

The derivation has to stop occupying the cooperative loop. This needs a maintainer decision, because each option adds an externally visible placement or resource choice under the "Decision authority" and "Ada interface values" sections of `AGENTS.md`:

- Run the derivation off the loop on a native thread, either by giving the management listener `Handler_Model => Flyology.Native_Task` at `server/src/flyology_object_storage_server_runtime.adb:853-858`, or by handing password verification to one dedicated native task. The first moves every management handler off the loop; the second keeps the handler cooperative and needs a hand-off point and a queue bound.
- Run it on a bounded worker pool sized for concurrent verifications. This bounds the CPU the login path can take, and the pool size is a new public resource value.
- Break the loop up with a cooperative yield and a `Cancellation.Requested` poll every N iterations at `server/src/flyology_object_storage_server_credentials.adb:134-147`. The interval N is a new value, the derivation still consumes the only loop, and a concurrent S3 request would be interleaved rather than freed.
- Grow the automatic pool at startup. That is a new public policy value and, as the `FLYOLOGY_LOOP_POOL_SIZE=8` measurement above shows, it only raises the number of concurrent logins needed to starve the loop.

The absent admission bound on the same route is a separate defect, reported as https://github.com/flyology-ada/flyology-object-storage/issues/70. The two are complementary: a rate limit bounds how many derivations can be queued, and moving the derivation off the loop bounds what one derivation costs the S3 listener. Fixing either alone leaves the other.

The regression test belongs in `server/tests/scripts/test.sh`, beside the existing login sequence at `:102-117`. The case should measure a signed S3 request with no other traffic, issue a valid login, and assert that a signed S3 request issued during the login stays within a bounded multiple of that baseline, with an absolute floor so scheduling noise on a sub-millisecond baseline cannot trip it. The spike is the test. No existing test asserts the wrong behaviour, so nothing has to change.

## Severity

Any deployment in which an administrator opens the workbench while S3 clients are active loses S3 service for the duration of the sign-in, about one second per login on this host, which is more than a hundred times the slowest baseline request measured beside it. It needs no invalid input and no unusual configuration: it is what the shipped defaults do on every sign-in. The unbounded form, ten or more simultaneous logins holding the listener for ten seconds or more, needs a local process that can reach the loopback-only management port, and it needs no session and no valid credential, because a wrong password costs the same derivation. A socket-only health check reports the server healthy throughout. Deterministic.

## Summary

Five management routes declare `Concurrency` and `Rate_Per_Second` admission policy, but the router is served with no middleware, so the only readers of those fields are never instantiated and the values have no effect. Fifty simultaneous `POST /api/login` requests from one peer with a wrong password are all admitted and all run a full 600,000-iteration derivation: fifty answers of 401 over 50.35 s, and not one 429 or 503. The declared policy on that route is `Concurrency => 1, Rate_Per_Second => 2`, whose token bucket has a burst capacity equal to the rate, so at most two should have been admitted.

## Problem

Commit `5fe6e3e`.

The login route declares a bucket of two requests per second and a concurrency of one (`server/src/flyology_object_storage_server_runtime.adb:882-887`):

```ada
      State.Routes.Post
        ("/api/login", Login'Access, Name => "admin.login",
         Policy =>
           (Routing.Default_Route_Policy with delta
              Body_Handling => Apps.Buffer_Body, Max_Body => 256,
              Concurrency => 1, Rate_Per_Second => 2));
```

Four more routes carry the same kind of policy: `/api/buckets` GET with `Concurrency => 2, Rate_Per_Second => 4` (`:890-894`), `/api/objects` with the same pair (`:895-899`), `/api/buckets` POST with `Concurrency => 1, Rate_Per_Second => 2` (`:900-906`), `/api/buckets/delete` with the same pair (`:907-913`), and `/api/logout` with `Concurrency => 4, Rate_Per_Second => 4` (`:914-918`).

The management `Handle` serves the router directly (`server/src/flyology_object_storage_server_runtime.adb:847-850`):

```ada
         HTTP.Configure_Ingress_Budget (Client, State.Budget'Access);
         State.Routes.Serve
           (State.Application, Client, Peer, Timeout => 30.0,
            Header_Timeout => 5.0, Token => Cancellation);
```

`rg -n -i 'middleware|bulkhead|rate_limit' server/src/` matches nothing, so no toolkit layer is installed anywhere in the crate.

The library states that the policy fields are read by those layers and by nothing else (pinned flyology_http 0.1.3-dev, `flyology-http-server-routing.ads:81-89`):

```ada
   --  Route-local policy consumed incrementally by optional toolkit layers.
   --  A zero concurrency or rate value means unlimited. CORS_Policy is an
   --  application-defined bounded registry slot interpreted by CORS
   --  middleware; zero means no CORS policy.
   --  @field Body_Handling Request body handling
   --  @field Max_Body Maximum decoded body bytes
   --  @field Timeout Deadline narrowing in seconds; negative preserves it
   --  @field Concurrency Maximum active handlers; zero is unlimited
   --  @field Rate_Per_Second Per-client admission rate; zero is unlimited
```

The two layers that would consume them are shipped and unused. `flyology-http-server-middleware_rate_limits.ads:24-25`:

```ada
   --  Token-bucket middleware. Burst capacity equals the configured route
   --  requests-per-second rate. Rejection is 429 with Retry-After.
```

and `flyology-http-server-middleware_bulkheads.ads:20`:

```ada
   --  Nonblocking bulkhead middleware. Rejection is 503 with Retry-After.
```

Because the library calls the consuming layers optional, this is not a violation of a library contract, and `server/README.md` promises no rate limit. What is wrong is that the executable writes admission policy on five routes, never enforces it, and paces its own test as though the limit existed (`server/tests/scripts/test.sh:102-107`):

```sh
  code=$(admin_request POST "$base/api/login" "$body" "$headers" \
    -H "Origin: $base" \
    --data-binary 'username=admin&password=000000000000000000000000000000000000000000000000')
  test "$code" = 401
  sleep 1
```

No assertion in that script expects a 429 or a 503, and the `sleep 1` between mutating requests passes with or without a limiter, so no existing test asserts the wrong behaviour and none has to change.

## What happens now

Spike: the program below starts the built server on a temporary root with `env -i` and ephemeral ports, then fires fifty barrier-synchronised `POST /api/login` requests with a wrong 48-hex password from one peer, tallies the outcomes and any `Retry-After` headers, and asserts the behaviour the written policy implies, that some requests are rejected with 429 or 503 and that at most the declared burst of two is admitted. The control issues one wrong-password login on its own.

```python
results, lock, gate = [], threading.Lock(), threading.Barrier(count)

def one():
    gate.wait()
    t0 = time.monotonic()
    try:
        c = http.client.HTTPConnection("127.0.0.1", port, timeout=600)
        c.request("POST", "/api/login", body=body,
                  headers={"Host": host, "Origin": f"http://{host}",
                           "Content-Type": "application/x-www-form-urlencoded",
                           "Content-Length": str(len(body))})
        r = c.getresponse(); r.read()
        retry = r.getheader("Retry-After")
        outcome = str(r.status) + (f" Retry-After={retry}" if retry else "")
```

Output, exit status 1:

```
  -- management port 59476; declared policy on /api/login is Concurrency => 1, Rate_Per_Second => 2
  -- 50 simultaneous POST /api/login with a wrong 48-hex password:
      50 x 401
     wall=50.35s min=1.00s median=26.34s max=50.35s
  -- fully processed by a handler (200/401): 50; rejected by a limiter (429/503): 0
  FAILED: a per-client rate limit or bulkhead rejects some of 50 simultaneous logins with 429/503
  -- burst capacity of the declared policy is 2; observed admissions: 50 (wall 50.35s)
  FAILED: a simultaneous burst from one peer admits at most the declared burst of 2
  -- control: one wrong-password login on its own: 401 {"authenticated":false}
  ok: control: a single wrong-password login is answered 401
BUG REPRODUCED: 2 failed expectation(s)
```

All fifty requests are outstanding at once, because the barrier releases them together, and all fifty are run to completion by a handler. The burst is not merely absorbed quickly: it costs 50.35 s of wall time, since each admitted request pays a full derivation on the single event loop, which is the separate defect reported as https://github.com/flyology-ada/flyology-object-storage/issues/69. The minimum latency of 1.00 s is one derivation, so the serialisation is the loop rather than a limiter.

Host: macOS 26.6.2, arm64, GNAT 16.1.0 via Alire.

## What should happen

Either the declared policy is enforced, so a burst from one peer is answered 429 with `Retry-After` or 503 as `flyology-http-server-middleware_rate_limits.ads:24-25` and `flyology-http-server-middleware_bulkheads.ads:20` document, or the routes stop declaring policy that nothing reads. A route that records `Concurrency => 1` and admits fifty concurrent handlers describes a protection the server does not have.

## Suggested treatment

Installing the shipped layers is the smaller change, and it needs a maintainer decision, because both take externally visible resource choices as generic formals under the "Decision authority" section of `AGENTS.md`:

- Instantiate `Middleware_Rate_Limits` over the router's own typed middleware package (`flyology-http-server-routing.ads:36-37`) and register it with `Add_Middleware` (`flyology-http-server-routing.ads:331`) where the routes are registered, so it is in place before `State.Routes.Serve` runs at `server/src/flyology_object_storage_server_runtime.adb:847-850`. Its `Client_Key` formal decides what identifies a client, direct peer address or a trusted-proxy header, and its `Capacity` formal bounds the retained attacker-controlled keys. Both are new policy for a listener that is always bound to loopback.
- Instantiate `Middleware_Bulkheads` as well if the `Concurrency` values are to take effect. Its `Global_Limit` and `Route_Capacity` formals are two more new bounds, and a global limit interacts with the listener's own 32 handler slots.
- Alternatively, drop the `Concurrency` and `Rate_Per_Second` fields from the five route registrations, so the source stops describing an admission bound that does not exist. This closes the discrepancy without adding a limit, and it leaves the login path with no bound at all, which matters because of https://github.com/flyology-ada/flyology-object-storage/issues/69.

The regression test belongs in `server/tests/scripts/test.sh`. If a limiter is installed, the case should fire a simultaneous burst of logins from one peer and assert that at least one response is 429 or 503 and carries `Retry-After`, and that the number fully processed does not exceed the declared burst. The spike is the test. If instead the fields are removed, the assertion is that no route registration carries a `Concurrency` or `Rate_Per_Second` value while no middleware is installed.

## Severity

The management listener has no admission bound of any kind, so any local process that can reach the loopback port can keep fifty full password derivations running, while the route registrations say a burst of two is the limit. On its own the effect is a discrepancy between the written policy and the behaviour, since the listener is loopback-only, the password is 192 bits and brute force stays infeasible. It matters because it is what leaves the expensive login path of https://github.com/flyology-ada/flyology-object-storage/issues/69 unbounded, turning a one-second stall into one that lasts as long as an attacker keeps posting. The `Concurrency => 1` intended to serialise bucket creation and deletion is equally absent, although the backends' own atomic primitives keep those operations safe. Deterministic.

## Summary

The executable checks only that `FLYOLOGY_OBJECT_STORAGE_ROOT` is non-empty and passes it straight to `Files.Open` or `SQLite.Open`, both of which treat the directory as a store they already own. The files backend recursively deletes a pre-existing `<root>/tmp` tree, and the SQLite backend deletes every ordinary file under `<root>/staging` and every ordinary file under `<root>/objects` that the freshly created catalog does not reference. Neither `Open` looks for any marker distinguishing a store from an arbitrary directory, and no layout marker is defined anywhere. An operator who mistypes the root loses that content silently and irreversibly, while the server prints `READY` and serves normally.

## Problem

Commit `5fe6e3e`.

The executable's only check on the root is that it is not empty (`server/src/flyology_object_storage_server_configuration.adb:42-46`):

```ada
      if Result.Backend /= Memory and then Root'Length = 0 then
         raise Constraint_Error with
           "FLYOLOGY_OBJECT_STORAGE_ROOT is required for " & Backend_Name;
      end if;
      Result.Storage_Root := To_Unbounded_String (Root);
```

and the value goes straight to the backend (`server/src/flyology_object_storage_server.adb:64-68`):

```ada
      when Configuration.Files =>
         declare
            Store : Flyology.Object_Storage.Backends.Files.Store :=
              Flyology.Object_Storage.Backends.Files.Open
                (US.To_String (Settings.Storage_Root));
```

with the same shape for SQLite (`server/src/flyology_object_storage_server.adb:78-82`).

`Files.Open` deletes a pre-existing `tmp` tree after checking only that it is not a symlink and is a directory (`src/flyology-object_storage-backends-files.adb:3549-3564`):

```ada
         if GNAT.OS_Lib.Is_Symbolic_Link
           (Join (US.To_String (Full), "tmp"))
         then
            raise Configuration_Error;
         elsif Ada.Directories.Exists (Join (US.To_String (Full), "tmp")) then
            if Ada.Directories.Kind (Join (US.To_String (Full), "tmp")) /=
              Ada.Directories.Directory
            then
               raise Configuration_Error;
            end if;
            Validate_Tree_Symlink_Free
              (Join (US.To_String (Full), "tmp"));
            Ada.Directories.Delete_Tree (Join (US.To_String (Full), "tmp"));
            Sync_Directory (Result, US.To_String (Full));
         end if;
         Ensure_Directory (Result, Join (US.To_String (Full), "tmp"));
```

`Validate_Tree_Symlink_Free` raises only for a depth above 64, a symlink, a missing or non-directory path, or an entry that is neither a directory nor an ordinary file (`src/flyology-object_storage-backends-files.adb:296-301,315-323`), so a tree of ordinary files and directories passes the check and is then deleted. The swept directory is exactly the store's staging area (`src/flyology-object_storage-backends-files.adb:364-365`):

```ada
   function Temp_Path (Item : Store) return String is
     (Join (Root_Directory (Item), "tmp"));
```

`SQLite.Open` sweeps two directories of its own (`sqlite/src/flyology-object_storage-backends-sqlite.adb:301-305`):

```ada
         Result.Lock.Handle := Acquire_Root_Lock
           (Join (US.To_String (Full), Lock_Name));
         Catalogs.Open (Result.Catalog, Catalog_Path (Result));
         Clean_Directory (Result, Staging_Path (Result), False);
         Clean_Directory (Result, Objects_Path (Result), True);
```

and `Clean_Directory` deletes every entry that a brand-new catalog does not reference (`sqlite/src/flyology-object_storage-backends-sqlite.adb:264-270`):

```ada
            if not Check_References
              or else not Is_Payload_Name (Name)
              or else not Catalogs.Payload_Referenced (Item.Catalog, Name)
            then
               Ada.Directories.Delete_File
                 (Ada.Directories.Full_Name (Directory_Entry));
            end if;
```

There is no layout marker for either backend to check. `docs/architecture/files-layout.md` states the precondition on the operator at `:3-4`:

```
The files backend requires exclusive ownership of its configured root by one
process.
```

and at `:65-67` says what opening does with it:

```
bucket directories are staged beneath `tmp/` and atomically renamed into the
namespace; opening the exclusively owned root removes interrupted staging
artifacts before accepting work.
```

Grepping that document for `marker`, `.flyology`, `layout file` or `sentinel` returns nothing: it defines no on-disk artifact that `Open` could require before treating a directory as its own. The SQLite root's `catalog.sqlite3` and `.flyology-object-storage.lock` are created by `Open`, not required by it, so they are not a marker either. The variable's documentation carries no precondition at all (`server/README.md:64`):

```
- `FLYOLOGY_OBJECT_STORAGE_ROOT` is mandatory for files and SQLite.
```

No existing test covers it. `server/tests/scripts/test.sh:458-471` always points each backend at a fresh `$RUN_ROOT/<backend>-store`, so no test opens a store on a directory that already holds unrelated content, and none has to change.

## What happens now

Spike, at the backend: the program below creates a scratch directory containing `tmp/keep.txt`, `tmp/nested/deep.txt` and `notes/readme.txt`, runs a control on a dedicated empty root by opening it and creating a bucket, then calls `Files.Open` on the foreign directory and asserts that `Open` either raised `Configuration_Error` or left the sentinels in place.

```ada
      declare
         Store : Files.Store := Files.Open (Foreign);
         pragma Unreferenced (Store);
      begin
         Note ("Files.Open (foreign root) returned normally");
      end;
   exception
      when Error : Files.Configuration_Error =>
         Refused := True;
...
   Check (Refused or else Ada.Directories.Exists (Keep),
          "Files.Open either refuses a root that is not a store or leaves " &
          "<root>/tmp/keep.txt in place");
```

Output, exit status 1, with the scratch directory names elided:

```
  -- foreign root = <scratch directory>
  --   before: tmp/keep.txt exists        = TRUE
  --   before: tmp/nested/deep.txt exists = TRUE
  --   before: notes/readme.txt exists    = TRUE
  -- control: dedicated empty root <scratch directory>
  --   Create_Bucket status = SUCCESS
  ok: control: a dedicated empty root opens and accepts a bucket
  -- Files.Open (foreign root) returned normally
  --   after:  tmp/keep.txt exists        = FALSE
  --   after:  tmp/nested/deep.txt exists = FALSE
  --   after:  notes/readme.txt exists    = TRUE
  --   Open refused the root (Configuration_Error) = FALSE
  FAILED: Files.Open either refuses a root that is not a store or leaves <root>/tmp/keep.txt in place
  FAILED: Files.Open either refuses a root that is not a store or leaves <root>/tmp/nested/deep.txt in place
  ok: control: a sibling directory the store does not use survives
BUG REPRODUCED: 2 failed expectation(s)
```

Spike, through the documented variable: a second program starts the built server itself with `FLYOLOGY_OBJECT_STORAGE_ROOT` pointed at prepared foreign directories, once for each persistent backend, waits for both `READY` lines, stops the server, and compares the directory listings.

```sh
  env -i PATH="$PATH" \
    FLYOLOGY_ADMIN_ASSET_ROOT="$assets" \
    FLYOLOGY_OBJECT_STORAGE_BACKEND="$backend" \
    FLYOLOGY_OBJECT_STORAGE_ROOT="$store" \
```

Output, exit status 1, with the scratch directory names elided:

```
  -- files root <scratch>/files-root before:
       ./notes/readme.txt
       ./tmp/keep.txt
       ./tmp/nested/deep.txt
  -- files server reached READY:
       READY s3 http://127.0.0.1:59782 backend=files
       READY admin http://127.0.0.1:59783/
  -- files root after:
       ./notes/readme.txt
  FAILED: files: the server refuses a root that is not a store, or leaves tmp/keep.txt in place
  FAILED: files: ... and leaves tmp/nested/deep.txt in place
  ok: control: files: a sibling directory the store does not use survives
  -- sqlite root <scratch>/sqlite-root before:
       ./notes/readme.txt
       ./objects/keep.txt
       ./staging/keep.txt
  -- sqlite server reached READY:
       READY s3 http://127.0.0.1:59789 backend=sqlite
       READY admin http://127.0.0.1:59790/
  -- sqlite root after:
       ./.flyology-object-storage.lock
       ./catalog.sqlite3
       ./notes/readme.txt
  FAILED: sqlite: the server refuses a root that is not a store, or leaves objects/keep.txt in place
  FAILED: sqlite: ... and leaves staging/keep.txt in place
  ok: control: sqlite: a sibling directory the store does not use survives
BUG REPRODUCED: 4 failed expectation(s)
```

The `notes/readme.txt` control survives both backends. That is what makes the loss hard to notice: the sweep is confined to the store's own directory names, so an operator inspecting the rest of the root sees nothing missing.

Host: macOS 26.6.2, arm64, GNAT 16.1.0 via Alire.

## What should happen

Opening a directory that has never been a store must either be refused with a named error, or must leave that directory's existing content in place. `docs/architecture/files-layout.md:3-4` states exclusive ownership as a precondition and `:65-67` describes the sweep as something done to an exclusively owned root, but nothing in the code establishes that the root at hand is one, and `server/README.md:64` documents the variable without the precondition.

## Suggested treatment

The fix needs a maintainer decision, both on where it sits and on whether it introduces a new on-disk artifact, which is a new externally visible precondition under the "Decision authority" section of `AGENTS.md`:

- Define a layout marker file. The first `Open` of an empty or absent root writes it, and every later `Open` requires it before treating the directory as a store, refusing with `Configuration_Error` otherwise. This is a new on-disk artifact, a new name in the root, and a new failure mode for a store created by an earlier version, which will not carry the marker.
- Refuse a root that already holds entries the layout does not define, so a directory containing `notes/` or an unexpected `tmp/` is rejected without any new artifact. This changes what `Open` accepts for existing deployments whose roots hold operator files beside the store.
- Validate the root in the executable before opening it, leaving both backends unchanged. This protects the documented variable but leaves a library caller with the same behaviour.

The choice also decides which layer changes. The destructive code is the backends' `Open` (`src/flyology-object_storage-backends-files.adb:3549-3564` and `sqlite/src/flyology-object_storage-backends-sqlite.adb:301-305`), while the reachable path from one documented, unvalidated variable is the executable's (`server/src/flyology_object_storage_server_configuration.adb:42-46` and `server/src/flyology_object_storage_server.adb:64-68,78-82`). A related asymmetry in the same two `Open` procedures is reported as https://github.com/flyology-ada/flyology-object-storage/issues/73.

The regression test belongs in two places. For the executable, add a case to `server/tests/scripts/test.sh` beside the per-backend loop at `:458-471` that points a files root at a directory holding `tmp/keep.txt` and a SQLite root at one holding `objects/keep.txt` and `staging/keep.txt`, and asserts that the server either fails to start or leaves those files in place. For the backend, `tests/src/files_open_probe.adb` already exists as the black-box opener used by `tests/scripts/test.sh:16-25` for the symlinked-staging case; a foreign-root case belongs beside it, asserting the same expectation directly against `Files.Open`. The spikes are the tests.

## Severity

An operator who mistypes the root, `/var` for `/var/lib/flyology`, a home directory that already has a `tmp`, or a checkout with a scratch area, loses that content with no diagnostic and no way to recover it, and the server reports itself ready. The data destroyed is never the store's own acknowledged writes, and it needs a root that is not a dedicated directory, which is an operator mistake rather than ordinary use. On macOS `/tmp` and `/var` are themselves symlinks, so a root spelled that way is refused by the root check at `src/flyology-object_storage-backends-files.adb:3526-3527` and a Linux deployment is the exposed case for those two names; the reproduction above uses ordinary directories instead, and the defect does not depend on the platform. Deterministic.

## Summary

When a child generation terminates with an exception, the supervisor hands back the child's identity and a bounded termination summary carrying the exception name and up to 512 characters of message, but the executable prints only `Result.Outcome'Image`. A management listener that cannot bind therefore produces exactly one line, `object-storage server stopped: STARTUP_FAILED`, and the exception never reaches GNAT's unhandled-exception reporter either. A port already in use, a permission problem, an `FLYOLOGY_S3_PORT` equal to `FLYOLOGY_ADMIN_PORT` and an exhausted restart budget all read the same, and the S3 child's earlier `READY` line makes the output look like a healthy start followed by an unexplained failure.

## Problem

Commit `5fe6e3e`.

The report discards every field but the outcome (`server/src/flyology_object_storage_server_runtime.adb:1074-1081`):

```ada
   Supervisors.Run (Supervisor, Context, Result);
   Flyology_Object_Storage_Server_Signals.Complete;
   if Result.Outcome /= Flyology.Supervision.Shutdown_Completed then
      Ada.Text_IO.Put_Line
        (Ada.Text_IO.Standard_Error,
         "object-storage server stopped: " & Result.Outcome'Image);
      Ada.Command_Line.Set_Exit_Status (Ada.Command_Line.Failure);
   end if;
```

`Result.Child`, `Result.Generation` and `Result.Termination` are never read, although the pinned flyology 0.1.1-dev release fills all of them (`flyology-supervision.ads:436-442`):

```ada
   type Supervisor_Result is record
      Outcome     : Supervisor_Outcome := Shutdown_Completed;
      Child       : Child_Id := Child_Id'First;
      Generation  : Supervision.Generation := Supervision.Generation'First;
      Termination : Termination_Summary;
      Incident    : Incident_Context := No_Incident;
   end record;
```

and the summary retains the exception name and message (`flyology-supervision.ads:177-191`):

```ada
   type Termination_Summary is record
      Kind           : Termination_Kind := No_Termination;
      Exception_Id   : Ada.Exceptions.Exception_Id :=
        Ada.Exceptions.Null_Id;
      Exception_Name_Length : Supervision.Exception_Name_Length := 0;
      Exception_Name_Truncated : Boolean := False;
      Exception_Name : String (1 .. Maximum_Exception_Name_Length) :=
        (others => ' ');
      Task_Id        : Ada.Task_Identification.Task_Id :=
        Ada.Task_Identification.Null_Task_Id;
      Message_Length : Diagnostic_Length := 0;
      Message_Truncated : Boolean := False;
      Message        : String (1 .. Maximum_Diagnostic_Length) :=
        (others => ' ');
   end record;
```

with `Maximum_Diagnostic_Length` of 512 (`flyology-supervision.ads:150`).

The management listener's bind has no handler, so `Socket_Error` escapes the generation and is what the supervisor classifies (`server/src/flyology_object_storage_server_runtime.adb:922-925`):

```ada
      Sockets.Bind_Socket
        (Listener, Sockets.Network_Endpoint
           (Sockets.Loopback_IPv4, Configuration.Admin_Port));
      Sockets.Listen_Socket (Listener, Length => 64);
```

The exception does not reach the main procedure's handler either, because `Runtime` catches everything and returns normally (`server/src/flyology_object_storage_server_runtime.adb:1082-1089`), and the supervisor outcome path never raises. The contrast is visible in the same executable: a failure before the supervisor starts does name its cause (`server/src/flyology_object_storage_server.adb:92-98`):

```ada
exception
   when Error : others =>
      Admin_Credentials.Wipe (Password);
      Ada.Text_IO.Put_Line
        (Ada.Text_IO.Standard_Error,
         "object-storage server startup failed: " &
         Ada.Exceptions.Exception_Information (Error));
      raise;
```

Nothing catches the conflict earlier, because `Configuration.Load` validates neither port availability nor an S3 port equal to the management port (`server/src/flyology_object_storage_server_configuration.adb:67-70`):

```ada
      Result.S3_Port := Flyology.IO.Sockets.Port'Value
        (Environment ("FLYOLOGY_S3_PORT", "9000"));
      Result.Admin_Port := Flyology.IO.Sockets.Port'Value
        (Environment ("FLYOLOGY_ADMIN_PORT", "9001"));
```

so the supervisor is the first place the conflict is observed.

No existing test asserts the wrong behaviour. `expect_startup_failure` checks only that the process exits non-zero (`server/tests/scripts/test.sh:343-353`):

```sh
expect_startup_failure() {
  local name=$1
  shift
  if env -i PATH="$PATH" \
    FLYOLOGY_ADMIN_CREDENTIALS_FILE="$RUN_ROOT/$name-admin.credentials" \
    "$@" "$SERVER" >"$RUN_ROOT/$name.log" 2>&1
  then
    echo "server unexpectedly accepted invalid configuration: $name" >&2
    exit 1
  fi
}
```

and every case it drives fails before the supervisor starts, in the path that does print `Exception_Information`. Nothing provokes a child-generation failure, so nothing has to change.

## What happens now

Spike: the program below holds a loopback port with a `python3` listener, starts the built executable with `FLYOLOGY_ADMIN_PORT` set to that port, captures stdout and stderr, and asserts that the failure diagnostic names at least one of the failing child, the endpoint it tried to bind, or the exception. It scopes the assertion to the failure report itself, because the `BOOTSTRAP ADMIN` banner and the credentials path are printed on every run and say nothing about the failure.

```sh
diagnostics=$(sed -n '/object-storage server stopped:/,$p' "$work/err")
...
if printf '%s' "$diagnostics" | grep -qi 'admin\|management\|workbench\|child'; then
  named_child=0
...
if printf '%s' "$diagnostics" | grep -q ":$busy"; then
  named_endpoint=0
...
if printf '%s' "$diagnostics" | grep -qi 'socket_error\|address already in use\|EADDRINUSE\|exception'; then
  named_exception=0
```

Output, exit status 1, with the scratch credentials path elided:

```
  -- another process holds 127.0.0.1:59950 (a port already in use is the common operator mistake)
  -- server exit status: 1
  -- stdout:
       READY s3 http://127.0.0.1:59957 backend=memory
  -- stderr:
       BOOTSTRAP ADMIN username=admin password=f84f1a225733d287b624d0f727bb3b21091afc80b516bf26
       Store this password now; it will not be shown again. credentials=<scratch>/admin.credentials
       object-storage server stopped: STARTUP_FAILED
  -- failure diagnostic under test:
       object-storage server stopped: STARTUP_FAILED
  ok: control: a management bind failure gives a non-zero exit status
  ok: control: the executable reports that the server stopped
  -- diagnostic names the failing child: no
  -- diagnostic names the endpoint 127.0.0.1:59950: no
  -- diagnostic names the exception: no
  FAILED: the startup-failure diagnostic names the failing child, the endpoint, or the exception
  -- control (pre-supervisor failure, bad backend name), stderr:
       raised CONSTRAINT_ERROR : FLYOLOGY_OBJECT_STORAGE_BACKEND must be memory, files, or sqlite
  ok: control: a configuration failure before the supervisor names its cause
BUG REPRODUCED: 1 failed expectation(s)
```

The password shown in that capture belongs to a throwaway server on a temporary root and is not a live credential. The controls establish both halves of the gap: the failure is detected, since the exit status is 1 and the stopped line is printed, and the executable is capable of naming a cause, since a configuration failure before the supervisor prints the exception text. The S3 child had already announced `READY s3 http://127.0.0.1:59957`, so the operator sees a listener come up and then an unexplained stop.

Host: macOS 26.6.2, arm64, GNAT 16.1.0 via Alire.

## What should happen

A failed start must name what failed. The supervisor already hands back the failing child, the generation and a bounded summary with the exception name and up to 512 characters of message (`flyology-supervision.ads:177-191,436-442`), so the operator diagnostic should distinguish a busy port from a permission problem, an asset failure or an exhausted restart budget.

## Suggested treatment

Extend the report at `server/src/flyology_object_storage_server_runtime.adb:1074-1081` to print `Result.Child`, `Result.Generation` and the retained termination text beside the outcome, using the summary's accessors so the fixed-length storage is trimmed to its meaningful prefix. Something of the shape

```ada
      Ada.Text_IO.Put_Line
        (Ada.Text_IO.Standard_Error,
         "object-storage server stopped: " & Result.Outcome'Image &
         " child=" & Result.Child'Image &
         " generation=" & Result.Generation'Image &
         " exception=" &
           Flyology.Supervision.Exception_Name_Text (Result.Termination) &
         " message=" &
           Flyology.Supervision.Message_Text (Result.Termination));
```

closes the defect without changing any interface value: the fields already exist, the two accessors that trim the fixed-length storage are already published at `flyology-supervision.ads:197-205`, and the executable already writes to standard error on this path. Two smaller choices are worth settling while making it. Whether the child identifiers are printed as their enumeral image or mapped to the names the `READY` lines use, `s3` and `admin`, is a presentation choice. Whether the same detail is printed for `Recovery_Exhausted` and `Failure_Escalated`, which take the identical path, is the other; printing it for every non-`Shutdown_Completed` outcome is the simplest and matches the existing condition.

The regression test belongs in `server/tests/scripts/test.sh`, beside `expect_startup_failure` at `:343-353`. The new case has to provoke a failure inside a child generation rather than before the supervisor, which the existing cases do not: hold a loopback port, start the server with `FLYOLOGY_ADMIN_PORT` set to it, and assert that stderr names the failing child, the endpoint, or the exception, in addition to the non-zero exit the existing helper checks. The spike is the test.

## Severity

Every distinct startup failure inside a child generation is reported as one enumeral, so an operator has no way to tell a busy port from a permission problem or an exhausted restart budget, and the preceding `READY s3` line points at the wrong listener. A port already in use is a common operator mistake, not an unusual input, and it is reached with the default configuration on a host where something else already holds port 9001. No data is wrong and nothing is unsafe: the process fails closed with a non-zero exit status. Deterministic.

## Summary

The executable offers one `FLYOLOGY_OBJECT_STORAGE_ROOT` variable for both persistent backends, and only one of them enforces the exclusive ownership the architecture document requires. `SQLite.Open` takes a non-blocking `flock` on a lock file in the root and refuses a second process with a named error; `Files.Open` takes no lock of any kind. A second server pointed at a live files root therefore starts, and its `Open` recursively deletes `<root>/tmp`, which is not a startup-only directory but the staging target the first, still running process uses for bucket creation, object publication, tag replacement and multipart completion.

## Problem

Commit `5fe6e3e`.

The SQLite backend acquires an exclusive root lock (`sqlite/src/flyology-object_storage-backends-sqlite.adb:107-118`):

```ada
   function Acquire_Root_Lock (Path : String) return System.Address is
      package CS renames Interfaces.C.Strings;
      Value  : CS.chars_ptr := CS.New_String (Path);
      Handle : System.Address;
   begin
      Handle := Acquire_Root_Lock_C (Value);
      CS.Free (Value);
      if Handle = System.Null_Address then
         raise Configuration_Error with
           "could not acquire exclusive SQLite backend root lock";
      end if;
      return Handle;
```

called from `Open` before anything is swept (`sqlite/src/flyology-object_storage-backends-sqlite.adb:301-305`):

```ada
         Result.Lock.Handle := Acquire_Root_Lock
           (Join (US.To_String (Full), Lock_Name));
         Catalogs.Open (Result.Catalog, Catalog_Path (Result));
         Clean_Directory (Result, Staging_Path (Result), False);
         Clean_Directory (Result, Objects_Path (Result), True);
```

`Files.Open` has no equivalent. Its whole body is `src/flyology-object_storage-backends-files.adb:3514-3573`, and the only guards are symlink and directory-kind checks before the sweep (`:3549-3564`):

```ada
         if GNAT.OS_Lib.Is_Symbolic_Link
           (Join (US.To_String (Full), "tmp"))
         then
            raise Configuration_Error;
         elsif Ada.Directories.Exists (Join (US.To_String (Full), "tmp")) then
            if Ada.Directories.Kind (Join (US.To_String (Full), "tmp")) /=
              Ada.Directories.Directory
            then
               raise Configuration_Error;
            end if;
            Validate_Tree_Symlink_Free
              (Join (US.To_String (Full), "tmp"));
            Ada.Directories.Delete_Tree (Join (US.To_String (Full), "tmp"));
            Sync_Directory (Result, US.To_String (Full));
         end if;
```

The directory that sweep removes is the live staging area, not a startup-only scratch space (`src/flyology-object_storage-backends-files.adb:364-365`):

```ada
   function Temp_Path (Item : Store) return String is
     (Join (Root_Directory (Item), "tmp"));
```

Ordinary operations stage inside it throughout the process lifetime: `Create_Bucket` at `:3606`, `Put_Bucket_Tags` at `:3902`, `Put_Object_Internal` at `:7102`, `Copy_Object` at `:7673`, `Put_Object_Tags` at `:9887`, `Put_Multipart_Part` at `:10884` and `Complete_Multipart_Upload` at `:11848`, among many more uses of `Temp_Path (Item)` in the same body. Retained multipart upload state is not in that tree, since uploads live under `buckets/<bucket>/multipart/` (`src/flyology-object_storage-backends-files.adb:402-403`), which the sweep does not touch. What a second `Open` can remove is a staged temporary that one of those publications is in the middle of writing, so the damage is bounded to transient staging.

The documents state exclusivity as a precondition on the operator and do not promise that the code enforces it. `docs/architecture/files-layout.md:3-4`:

```
The files backend requires exclusive ownership of its configured root by one
process.
```

`README.md:36` repeats it as a property of the backend, "for one process owning an exclusive storage root", and `docs/architecture/files-layout.md:85` treats a breach as the operator's:

```
concurrent external mutation violates the backend's exclusive-root contract.
```

So the files backend is not breaking a documented promise of enforcement. What makes this a defect is that the executable presents one variable for both persistent backends and enforces the precondition for exactly one of them, so the same operator mistake is refused with a named error on SQLite and silently accepted on files.

Nothing tests it: `server/tests/scripts/test.sh` never starts two servers on one root, so no existing test asserts the wrong behaviour and none has to change.

## What happens now

Spike: the program below starts one server on a fresh files root, places a sentinel file inside the live `<root>/tmp/`, starts a second server on the same root, and asserts that the second is refused or that the sentinel survives. The control repeats the identical two-process attempt on a SQLite root.

```sh
echo "sentinel: transient staging artefact of the live owning process" \
  >"$fstore/tmp/owner-staging.tmp"
...
p2=$(launch files "$fstore" "$work/f2.cred" "$work/f2.log"); pids="$pids $p2"
if settle "$work/f2.log" "$p2"; then f2_started=0; else f2_started=1; fi
...
check "$([ $f2_started -ne 0 ] && echo 0 || echo 1)" \
  "files: a second server is refused a root a live server already owns"
check "$survived" \
  "files: ... or at least leaves the live root's tmp/ contents in place"
```

Output, exit status 1, with the scratch directory names elided:

```
  -- server 1 owns <scratch>/files-root:
       READY s3 http://127.0.0.1:60041 backend=files
       READY admin http://127.0.0.1:60042/
  -- placed <scratch>/files-root/tmp/owner-staging.tmp while server 1 is live
  -- server 2 on the same root:
       READY s3 http://127.0.0.1:60048 backend=files
       READY admin http://127.0.0.1:60049/
  -- server 2 reached READY: yes
  -- server 1's tmp/owner-staging.tmp survived: no
  -- server 1 still alive: yes
  FAILED: files: a second server is refused a root a live server already owns
  FAILED: files: ... or at least leaves the live root's tmp/ contents in place
  -- control, sqlite: second server on the same root:
       object-storage server startup failed: raised FLYOLOGY.OBJECT_STORAGE.BACKENDS.SQLITE.CONFIGURATION_ERROR : could not acquire exclusive SQLit
       raised FLYOLOGY.OBJECT_STORAGE.BACKENDS.SQLITE.CONFIGURATION_ERROR : could not acquire exclusive SQLite backend root lock
  ok: control: sqlite refuses a second server on a root a live server owns
  ok: control: the sqlite refusal names the reason
BUG REPRODUCED: 2 failed expectation(s)
```

The sentinel stands in for the first process's transient staging artifacts and is created by the program. What the run demonstrates is that a second process empties a directory a live process owns, and that the first process keeps running unaware. It does not demonstrate destruction of a real in-flight staging file, which needs a timing race that was not attempted.

Host: macOS 26.6.2, arm64, GNAT 16.1.0 via Alire.

## What should happen

The same operator mistake should have the same outcome for both persistent backends the executable offers. A second process pointed at a files root that a live process already owns should be refused with a named error, as `sqlite/src/flyology-object_storage-backends-sqlite.adb:107-118` refuses it, rather than starting and emptying the first process's staging directory.

## Suggested treatment

The smallest fix is to give `Files.Open` the lock the SQLite backend already has: take `flock(LOCK_EX | LOCK_NB)` on a lock file in the root at the start of `src/flyology-object_storage-backends-files.adb:3514-3573`, before the `tmp` sweep at `:3549-3564`, and raise `Configuration_Error` with a named message when it cannot be acquired. The C helper the SQLite backend imports and the `Root_Lock` finalization that releases the handle are both already written, so this is a small change in mechanism.

It still needs a maintainer decision, because it puts a new artifact and a new failure mode into the files layout under the "Decision authority" section of `AGENTS.md`:

- A lock file inside the root, matching `.flyology-object-storage.lock` for SQLite. It adds a name in the root that `docs/architecture/files-layout.md` has to describe, and it makes the files backend fail to open on filesystems without working advisory locks, where it opens today.
- A lock file outside the root, chosen by the caller or the executable. This keeps the layout unchanged, at the cost of a new configuration surface and of a lock that does not travel with the store.
- No lock, and instead a refusal to sweep `<root>/tmp` when it is not empty, so a live process's staging is never removed by a second opener. This is weaker: two processes would still write to one root.

The choice interacts with the layout marker discussed in https://github.com/flyology-ada/flyology-object-storage/issues/71, since a marker file and a lock file could be the same artifact or two different ones, and both change what an `Open` requires of a directory.

The regression test belongs in `server/tests/scripts/test.sh`, which already starts a server per backend at `:458-471`: a case that starts a second server on the same files root while the first is live and asserts that it is refused, mirroring the SQLite control the reproduction above already passes. At the backend level, `tests/src/files_open_probe.adb` is the existing black-box opener, used by `tests/scripts/test.sh:16-25` for the symlinked-staging case; a second-opener case belongs beside it, asserting that a second `Files.Open` on a root an open `Store` still holds raises `Configuration_Error`. The spike is the test.

## Severity

A second server pointed at a live files root starts silently and empties the running process's staging directory, which is reached by an overlapping restart, a misconfigured supervisor unit, or an operator starting a second instance on a different port. The missing refusal is deterministic and so is the emptying. The harm beyond that is narrower: the destroyed data is transient staging rather than acknowledged writes, multipart upload state under `buckets/<bucket>/multipart/` is not touched, and losing a staged record that the first process is mid-way through publishing needs a timing race that was not demonstrated. The same mistake on a SQLite root is already refused with a named error. Files backend only.

## Summary

Two workflow steps pipe a conformance script into `tee` under GitHub's default `bash -e` shell, which sets no `pipefail`, so the step takes `tee`'s exit status and a failing script can never fail the job. Independently, the servers those scripts start are reachable only on the host loopback, while the s5cmd oracle runs in a container reaching `host.docker.internal`, which on a Linux runner is the docker0 gateway and refuses the connection. In the scheduled run 34031107803 of 2026-09-06 all three affected jobs report success while their logs show the very first s5cmd call failing with `connection refused`. The s5cmd byte and deletion oracles that the repository presents as its stock-client evidence have therefore never run in CI.

## Problem

Commit `5fe6e3e`.

The black-box step (`.github/workflows/ci.yml:268-272`):

```yaml
      - name: Run digest-pinned s5cmd oracle against memory backend
        run: |
          mkdir -p obj/ci
          ./tests/scripts/test-flyology-server.sh memory \
            2>&1 | tee obj/ci/s3-smoke.log
```

and the reference-smoke step, matrixed over `rustfs` and `seaweedfs` (`.github/workflows/ci.yml:299-305`):

```yaml
      - name: Run bounded permissive-reference slice
        env:
          FLYOLOGY_S3_SERVER_RUNNER: ${{ github.workspace }}/tests/scripts/run-s3-server-slice.sh
        run: |
          mkdir -p obj/ci
          ./tests/scripts/test-${{ matrix.reference }}.sh \
            2>&1 | tee obj/ci/reference-${{ matrix.reference }}.log
```

`grep -n 'shell:\|pipefail' .github/workflows/ci.yml` prints nothing, so both steps run under GitHub's default `bash -e {0}`. Without `pipefail` a pipeline's status is its last command's, which is `tee`, and `tee` succeeds whenever it can write the log.

The second cause is the endpoint. The three reference servers are published on the host loopback only:

```sh
tests/scripts/test-minio.sh:26:  --publish "127.0.0.1::9000" \
tests/scripts/test-rustfs.sh:29:  --publish "127.0.0.1::9000" \
tests/scripts/test-seaweedfs.sh:34:  --publish "127.0.0.1::8333" \
```

and the Flyology test server binds loopback directly rather than publishing anything (`tests/src/s3_server_harness.adb:120-121`):

```ada
   Sockets.Bind_Socket
     (Listener, Sockets.Network_Endpoint (Sockets.Loopback_IPv4, Port));
```

The client, however, is a sibling container told to reach the host gateway (`tests/scripts/run-s3-server-slice.sh:59-66`):

```sh
s5cmd() {
  docker run --rm --add-host host.docker.internal:host-gateway \
    --volume "$PAYLOAD_ROOT:/data:ro" \
    --env "AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID=$ACCESS_KEY" \
    --env "AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY=$SECRET_KEY" \
    --env AWS_REGION=us-east-1 \
    "$S5CMD_IMAGE" --retry-count 0 --endpoint-url "$ORACLE_ENDPOINT" "$@"
}
```

with the same `--add-host` in the full corpus runner (`tests/scripts/run-s3-implementation.sh:38-44`):

```sh
s5cmd() {
  docker run --rm --add-host host.docker.internal:host-gateway \
    --env "AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID=$ACCESS_KEY" \
    --env "AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY=$SECRET_KEY" \
    --env AWS_REGION=us-east-1 \
    "$S5CMD_IMAGE" --endpoint-url "$ORACLE_ENDPOINT" "$@"
}
```

and the endpoint passed to that runner is built on that name (`tests/scripts/test-flyology-server.sh:92-96`):

```sh
if ! env \
  FLYOLOGY_S3_IMPLEMENTATION="flyology-$BACKEND" \
  FLYOLOGY_S3_SERVER_REVISION="$SERVER_REVISION" \
  "$RUNNER" "http://host.docker.internal:$PORT" \
  "flyology-$BACKEND-slice-$$" "$ACCESS_KEY" "$SECRET_KEY"
```

On a Linux runner `host-gateway` is the docker0 address 172.17.0.1, which nothing is listening on, because the port was published to 127.0.0.1.

Both causes are visible in the scheduled run 34031107803 of 2026-09-06, on `main`, triggered by schedule. The `Reference smoke (rustfs)` job log records the first s5cmd call:

```
2026-09-06T11:45:26.6251385Z ERROR "mb s3://flyology-rustfs-corpus-2326": RequestError: send request failed caused by: Put "http://host.docker.internal:32768/flyology-rustfs-corpus-2326": dial tcp 172.17.0.1:32768: connect: connection refused
```

and the `S3 black-box smoke (memory + s5cmd)` job log records the same failure and the script's own diagnosis:

```
2026-09-06T12:06:21.3598338Z ERROR "mb s3://flyology-memory-slice-2648": RequestError: send request failed caused by: Put "http://host.docker.internal:37319/flyology-memory-slice-2648": dial tcp 172.17.0.1:37319: connect: connection refused
2026-09-06T12:06:21.4264723Z PORT 37319
2026-09-06T12:06:21.4266741Z Flyology memory endpoint runner failed
```

`Flyology memory endpoint runner failed` is the message the script prints immediately before `exit 1` (`tests/scripts/test-flyology-server.sh:97-101`):

```sh
then
  cat "$SERVER_LOG" >&2
  echo "Flyology $BACKEND endpoint runner failed" >&2
  exit 1
fi
```

`gh run view 34031107803` nonetheless reports every one of the three jobs as passing:

```
✓ main CI · 34031107803
Triggered via schedule about 18 hours ago
...
✓ Reference smoke (rustfs) in 15s (ID 101480737338)
✓ Reference smoke (seaweedfs) in 19s (ID 101480737386)
✓ S3 black-box smoke (memory + s5cmd) in 8m33s (ID 101482308887)
```

The 15 and 19 second durations are the whole reference jobs, image pull included, against a nominal 30-minute budget.

The claim the jobs are supposed to support is published (`docs/qualification/continuous-integration.md:25-28`):

```
Every pull request and push also runs the digest-pinned, MIT-licensed s5cmd
black-box corpus against the memory server. A scheduled lane runs a bounded
copy of the same wire slice against the digest-pinned Apache-2.0 RustFS and
SeaweedFS references.
```

Neither of those runs has happened on a Linux runner. Nothing in the repository tests the workflow for this. The maintained static workflow checker `tools/ci/verify-workflow-policy.sh`, run from `tools/ci/check-repository.sh:41-42` with its negative-oracle suite `tools/ci/test-workflow-policy.sh`, asserts action pinning, credential persistence, secret use and exact tool versions, and has no rule about piped steps or step-status propagation.

There is a second consequence, which the masked jobs would have surfaced. Run on macOS, where Docker forwards the loopback publication so the corpus does reach the server, `tests/scripts/test-minio.sh`, `test-rustfs.sh` and `test-seaweedfs.sh` all fail at the same point, the corpus's third HeadObject case (`tests/src/s3_implementation_corpus.adb:1144-1157`):

```ada
         declare
            Parameters : Low_Level.Head_Object_Parameters;
         begin
            Parameters.Version_ID := US.To_Unbounded_String ("null");
            Parameters.Request_Payer := US.To_Unbounded_String ("requester");
            Parameters.Checksum_Mode := True;
```

with `raised FLYOLOGY.OBJECT_STORAGE.CLIENT.LOW_LEVEL.INVALID_RESPONSE : HeadObject response does not match prepared request`. That is a client-side defect, reported separately as https://github.com/flyology-ada/flyology-object-storage/issues/39, and it is not caused by the workflow. It is relevant here because it is a real, reproducible third-party interop failure that the scheduled reference jobs are meant to catch and did not, since they never reached a server and could not have failed if they had.

## What happens now

Spike: the shell program below runs the workflow step's verbatim shape around a script that exits 1, first under GitHub's default `bash -e` and then, as a control, under `bash -eo pipefail`; asks Docker what `--publish 127.0.0.1::9000` actually binds; and greps the workflow for `shell:` or `pipefail`. It asserts the correct behaviour, that a step written the way the workflow writes it fails when the script fails.

```sh
printf '#!/bin/sh\necho "Flyology memory endpoint runner failed" >&2\nexit 1\n' >"$script"
...
# 1. The workflow step, verbatim shape, under GitHub's default shell.
bash -e -c "$script 2>&1 | tee /dev/null" >/dev/null 2>&1
...
# 2. Control: the same step with pipefail (what 'shell: bash' would give).
bash -eo pipefail -c "$script 2>&1 | tee /dev/null" >/dev/null 2>&1
...
cid=$(docker run --detach --rm --publish 127.0.0.1::9000 alpine:3 sleep 30 2>/dev/null || true)
mapping=$(docker port "$cid" 9000/tcp 2>/dev/null || true)
```

Output, exit status 1:

```
  -- default shell 'bash -e', script exits 1 through '| tee': step status 0
  FAILED: a failing conformance script fails the CI step as written (bash -e, pipe to tee)
  -- control 'bash -eo pipefail': step status 1
  ok: control: with pipefail the same step fails
  -- docker port for '--publish 127.0.0.1::9000': 127.0.0.1:32803
  -- the published port is bound to the host loopback only
  FAILED: ci.yml sets 'shell: bash' or pipefail for the piped steps
BUG REPRODUCED: 2 failed expectation(s)
```

A script that exits 1 through `| tee` gives the step status 0 under the default shell and status 1 with `pipefail`, and the publication is bound to 127.0.0.1 only, which is what the docker0 gateway address in the CI logs cannot reach.

Host: macOS 26.6.2, arm64, GNAT 16.1.0 via Alire. The published-port demonstration used the local Docker daemon; the jobs it describes run on `ubuntu-latest`.

## What should happen

A failing conformance script must fail its CI step, and the s5cmd container must be able to reach the server it is pointed at on a Linux runner, so that the black-box corpus and the reference slice actually execute the runs `docs/qualification/continuous-integration.md:25-28` says they execute.

## Suggested treatment

Two independent changes are needed, and fixing only the first turns the jobs red without making them meaningful, since the endpoint would still be unreachable.

For the step status, add `shell: bash` to the two steps at `.github/workflows/ci.yml:268-272` and `:299-305`; GitHub runs that shell as `bash --noprofile --norc -eo pipefail {0}`, so the pipeline takes the script's status. Adding `set -o pipefail` as the first line of each `run:` block has the same effect and is a smaller diff.

For the endpoint, the address is a maintainer decision, because each option changes what the runner exposes and to whom:

- Publish the reference servers on all interfaces instead of `127.0.0.1` (`tests/scripts/test-minio.sh:26`, `test-rustfs.sh:29`, `test-seaweedfs.sh:34`) and bind the Flyology harness beyond `Loopback_IPv4` (`tests/src/s3_server_harness.adb:120-121`), so the docker0 gateway address the client already uses resolves to a listening socket. This exposes the test server on the runner's other interfaces for the life of the job.
- Run the s5cmd container with `--network host` instead of `--add-host host.docker.internal:host-gateway` (`tests/scripts/run-s3-server-slice.sh:60`, `tests/scripts/run-s3-implementation.sh:39`) and point it at `127.0.0.1`, keeping the loopback publication. Host networking behaves differently on macOS, so the local and CI paths would diverge.
- Put the server and the client on the same user-defined Docker network and address the server by container name. This does not work for the Flyology harness, which is a host process rather than a container.

The regression test belongs in the maintained workflow checker `tools/ci/verify-workflow-policy.sh`, with its rejection fixture in `tools/ci/test-workflow-policy.sh`, both already run by `tools/ci/check-repository.sh:41-42`. The rule to add is that a `run:` block containing a pipeline must be accompanied by `shell: bash` or by `set -o pipefail`, with a mutation fixture that strips it and must be rejected. The spike is the test for the step-status half; the endpoint half is only observable in a job that actually runs, so its evidence is the first passing `mb s3://...` in the job log. No existing check asserts the wrong behaviour, so nothing has to change.

## Severity

The repository's own interop gate cannot fail. Three scheduled jobs report success on runs in which the conformance script demonstrably failed at its first request, so a maintainer reading a green run is told something untrue, and no interop regression on the s5cmd byte, deletion or restart routes can be caught in CI. It is not a latent risk: it has already hidden a real third-party failure, the one reported as https://github.com/flyology-ada/flyology-object-storage/issues/39, which reproduces on every one of the three reference servers. Both causes are static and reproduce on every Linux runner, and the black-box step also runs on every pull request and push, not only on the schedule. Deterministic.
